● I read the full 71-page paper (via text extraction, not sampling) and the
  website. Here's the real picture — problem, solution, and my honest read on
  whether this is fictional or substantive.

  The problem (this part is real and well-documented)

  The energy grid was built for one-way, predictable power flow: a few big
  plants → passive consumers. That model is breaking down because of three
  converging trends, all backed by IEA data in the paper:

  - Demand is exploding: global electricity demand could nearly double by 2050;
  EVs, AI data centers, heat pumps, and air conditioning are driving
  unprecedented, weather-sensitive peaks.
  - Generation is fragmenting: rooftop solar alone is projected to grow 429% by
  2035 (to ~3,700 GW). Millions of small, variable, distributed resources (solar
  panels, home batteries, EVs) now need to be coordinated instead of just a few
  big power plants.
  - Transactions are becoming micro and bidirectional: instead of one-way
  billing, you now have peer-to-peer trades, demand-response rebates, and
  battery-sharing — all requiring trust and verification at a much smaller,
  faster scale.

  The costs of not solving this are concrete and cited: blackouts cost
  ~$100B/year globally; grid congestion cost the US ~$21B in 2022 and Germany
  €4B; negative electricity pricing hours in Southern California exceeded 20% in
  2024 because surplus solar can't be intelligently redirected. The root cause
  the paper identifies isn't a lack of technology — it's fragmentation: every
  EV-charging platform, DER management tool, and subsidy system is a walled-off
  silo that can't talk to the others, and a lot of verification (installations,
  certifications, ownership) still happens on paper.

  The proposed solution: Digital Energy Grid (DEG)

  DEG is explicitly not a product, app, or piece of software — the paper repeats
  this several times. It's a proposed protocol layer (like TCP/IP for the
  internet, or HTML for the web) that would sit on top of the physical grid and
  let all these fragmented systems interoperate, built on three building blocks:

  1. Universal identity — every energy asset (a solar panel, an EV, a battery, a
  power plant) gets a globally unique, portable ID, like a phone number.
  2. Machine-readable data — specs, certifications, consumption data expressed
  in standard digital formats instead of PDFs/paper, so software (and AI) can
  act on it directly.
  3. Cryptographic verifiability — tamper-proof, portable records so trust
  doesn't require manual inspection or a middleman.

  On top of that, it envisions use cases like: automated solar-subsidy approval,
  city-wide demand-response programs with real-time rebates, households renting
  out spare home-battery capacity to utilities ("cloud energy storage"),
  cross-border carbon-credit trading, vehicle-to-grid EV discharge markets, and
  peer-to-peer solar trading between neighbors. Important honesty check: the
  paper itself labels these as hypothetical, illustrative scenarios with
  fictional names (Mihir, Emma, Sophia) — they are not case studies of things
  that have actually happened yet.

  Is this real or fictional? My honest assessment

  Split this into two questions:

  Is the problem real? Yes, unambiguously. It's backed by IEA (International
  Energy Agency) data throughout, and IEA co-authored two chapters and its
  Executive Director wrote a foreword. Grid coordination strain from
  decentralized renewables and EVs is one of the most discussed real problems in
  energy policy right now.

  Does the solution (DEG) actually exist? Not yet, as a built system — and the
  paper is upfront about that ("a vision, not a definitive solution"). But it's
  not vaporware/fantasy either, for a specific reason: this is being proposed by
  FIDE (Foundation for Interoperability in Digital Economy), founded by Nandan
  Nilekani, the person behind India's Aadhaar digital-identity system and a key
  architect of UPI (India's real-time payments protocol) and ONDC (open commerce
  network). The paper leans heavily on the fact that this exact pattern — open
  protocol instead of closed platform — already worked at massive scale in other
  domains: UPI (14.4 billion transactions/month in India), Brazil's Pix (75% of
  the population), Estonia's X-Road. That's the credibility basis: "we've done
  this before, in payments and commerce, now let's do it for energy."

  There is one concrete, real (if early) implementation already underway: the
  Unified Energy Interface (UEI), built on Beckn Protocol, for EV charging, P2P
  energy trading, and demand response — described in the paper as "beginning to
  take shape." The website also lists a real hackathon (Nov 22–25, 2025, London)
  and summit (Nov 28, 2025, King's College) around this. So it's moved from
  pure paper-concept to early pilot/community-building stage, but it is nowhere
  near proven at grid scale yet.

  Bottom line: this isn't a fictional idea in the sense of being speculative
  fluff — it's a serious, IEA-endorsed infrastructure proposal from a team with
  a real track record of shipping exactly this kind of protocol before
  (UPI/ONDC). But it's also not a "real, working thing" yet in the energy sector
  — it's at the stage UPI was before it launched: a compelling architectural
  bet on a genuinely painful, well-documented coordination problem, not yet
  validated at scale.

● Good question — let me clarify that, since it's easy to conflate "founded by
  an Indian" with "an Indian government project."

  Is this an India project?

  Not officially — no government of India is behind it. Here's the actual
  structure:

  - FIDE (Foundation for Interoperability in Digital Economy) is the
  organization that wrote most of the paper (chapters 3–8). It's a non-profit,
  not a government body — it describes itself as global in mission.
  - It was co-founded by Nandan Nilekani — an Indian entrepreneur (Infosys
  co-founder) who is the person behind India's Aadhaar (digital ID for 1.4B
  people), a key architect of UPI (India's instant-payments rail), and involved
  in ONDC (India's open commerce network). So the pedigree and playbook is
  deeply Indian.
  - Beckn Protocol, the underlying open-protocol technology DEG is meant to run
  on, also originated in India and powers ONDC.
  - IEA (International Energy Agency) — a Paris-based intergovernmental body,
  not Indian — co-authored two chapters and gave it real institutional weight.
  Fatih Birol (IEA's Executive Director, Turkish) wrote a foreword.

  So the honest framing: India isn't the sponsor, but India is the 
  proof-of-concept. The entire pitch of the paper is "we already built this
  pattern in India for payments (UPI) and commerce (ONDC), and it worked at a
  scale nobody thought possible — let's do the same thing for energy, globally."
  Every major precedent example in the paper is Indian (UPI, ONDC, PAHAL
  subsidy transfers), plus a couple of non-Indian ones (Brazil's Pix, Estonia's
  X-Road). The ambition is explicitly global — it's pitched to "governments,
  industries, innovators, communities" worldwide, not just India — but the
  people, the protocol, and the working examples are Indian in origin.

  The whole paper in plain terms

  Think of it like this:

  The setup: The old electricity grid is like an old-school phone system — one
  direction, one operator, everything centrally controlled and predictable.
  That's breaking. Now you've got millions of rooftop solar panels, home
  batteries, and electric cars that can both consume and produce power, and
  everyone needs to talk to everyone else in real time. The grid wasn't built
  for that.

  The pain point: Right now, all the tools trying to manage this are
  disconnected apps that don't talk to each other. Your solar subsidy
  application, your bank loan, your net-metering registration, your utility's
  demand-response app — none of them share data. So everything is slow, manual,
  paper-heavy, and full of duplicate verification. Multiply that friction by
  millions of devices and you get blackouts, wasted solar power (literally
  paying people not to generate it because the grid can't absorb it), and
  billions of dollars in delays.

  The idea (DEG): Instead of building yet another app, build a shared "language"
  that lets every app, device, and organization in energy plug into each other
  automatically — the same way the internet doesn't care what device you're on
  because everyone agreed on TCP/IP and HTML. Concretely, that shared language
  has 3 parts:
  1. Every device/asset gets an ID (like a phone number for your solar panel or
  EV battery).
  2. Data about that device (specs, certifications, how much it produced) is in
  a standard digital format any computer can read — not a PDF a human has to
  open.
  3. That data is cryptographically signed so it can't be faked, and it's
  portable — you own it and can carry it anywhere, not locked in one company's
  app.

  What that unlocks (the paper's example stories): a farmer gets solar subsidy,
  loan approval, and installer verification in one seamless flow instead of
  three separate bureaucracies; a city automatically pays people small rebates
  for nudging their thermostat during a heatwave to avoid a blackout; a
  homeowner rents out spare battery capacity to the utility overnight and gets
  paid automatically; a solar farm's carbon credits get verified and sold to a
  buyer on the other side of the planet without paperwork; your neighbor's
  excess solar gets sold directly to your EV charger.

  Where AI fits: two-way relationship — DEG gives AI clean, structured,
  trustworthy energy data to work with (AI needs good data to forecast demand,
  detect grid problems, etc.), and AI in turn helps run DEG (forecasting,
  anomaly detection, letting you tell a voice assistant "minimize my power bill"
  and having it actually go negotiate and execute trades for you).

  What it's honest about: this is a vision paper, not an announcement of a
  finished system. All the user stories (Mihir, Emma, Sophia, etc.) are
  explicitly labeled fictional/illustrative. It's not a silver bullet — you
  still need real grid upgrades, real hardware, real policy reform. DEG is just
  the "connective tissue" layer that could let all of that work together instead
  of in silos.

  Where it stands today: there's one real early build-out — the Unified Energy 
  Interface (UEI), running on Beckn Protocol, for EV charging and
  demand-response — described as "beginning to take shape." Plus an active
  hackathon/summit in London in November 2025 to build momentum. So: real
  problem, credible team with a proven playbook, early-stage execution — not yet
  a proven, deployed reality.

I've now read this second PDF fully too. It's a very different kind of document — and the connection to DEG is real and fairly direct.

What this second PDF actually is

This is not a think-piece or vision paper. It's an official Indian government document: a draft "National Electricity Data Sharing Framework, 2026", issued by India's Ministry of Power (dated June 2026), circulated to MEITY, the Central Electricity Regulatory Commission, all state electricity regulators, power utilities (DISCOMs/Gencos), industry bodies (FICCI, CII), and academic institutions — asking them to submit comments within 30 days. This is a real regulatory consultation process, the kind of thing governments do before finalizing a binding policy.

In plain terms: India's electricity data today (who's generating what, who's consuming what, outage data, pricing, grid status) is scattered across dozens of agencies — generators, transmission companies, state DISCOMs, regulators — in inconsistent formats, with no common way to access or share it. This draft framework proposes to fix that by creating:
- A National Electricity Data Centre (NEDC) and National Electricity Data Portal (NEDP) — a central place where standardized, machine-readable electricity datasets get published.
- A tiered classification system (Tier 1 "Public" data anyone can access freely; Tier 2 "Access Controlled" data requiring registration/KYC — e.g., feeder-level outage data, de-identified smart meter data).
- Rules for metadata, APIs, de-identification/anonymization of personal data (to comply with India's Digital Personal Data Protection Act), grievance redressal, and cybersecurity.
- A long appendix listing ~66 specific datasets (installed capacity, generation output, transmission line loading, DISCOM losses, rooftop solar stats, tariffs, etc.) that should be published.

The direct connection to DEG

This is the strongest, most concrete link I could ask for — the document explicitly names the bridge:

"India Energy Stack (IES)" — defined in the framework's glossary as "Foundational Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI) launched by the Ministry of Power to unify, digitize, and modernize India's power sector." This is India's real, government-led initiative that is functionally India's national attempt at exactly what DEG describes conceptually. The framework repeatedly says APIs and consent mechanisms should be "aligned with the India Energy Stack" — meaning this data-sharing framework is one building block of a bigger national DEG-style effort already underway inside the Indian government.

Concretely, mapping DEG's three pillars onto this document:

┌───────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│     DEG's pillar      │                                        How this framework implements it                                         │
├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Universal identity    │ Every "Data Issuer" (generator, DISCOM, regulator) is a defined, registered entity with roles/responsibilities; │
│                       │  datasets get standardized identifiers and a designated "Data Governance Officer"                               │
├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                       │ Explicitly required — Section 7.3 mandates metadata (schema, format, update cycle) for every dataset; Section   │
│ Machine-readable data │ 7.6 requires "standardised machine-readable format" within 18 months; interoperability is literally defined in  │
│                       │ the doc as "capability of different datasets to be machine-readable, communicable through secure APIs"          │
├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Verifiability /       │ Handled through DPDP Act compliance, anonymization/pseudonymization/tokenization techniques, audit trails, and  │
│ consent / portability │ a "consent mechanism aligned with the India Energy Stack" — echoing DEG's Mihir example where his solar         │
│                       │ telemetry is shared "with Mihir's digital consent"                                                              │
└───────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

It also reuses other pieces of India's existing digital-public-infrastructure toolkit that the DEG paper cites as precedent (UPI-style thinking) — API Setu, NAPIX, and IUDX (India's urban data exchange) are all explicitly named as the technical rails this electricity framework will ride on.

Where it's narrower than DEG (important nuance)

DEG's vision has three coordination layers: information flows, energy flows, and transaction flows — plus a fully decentralized "network of networks" built on an open protocol (Beckn), closer to peer-to-peer.

This framework covers only the information-flow layer — it's purely about data classification, publication, and access. It says nothing about energy transactions (buying/selling power), peer-to-peer trading, or coordinating the physical flow of electricity — the parts of DEG's vision covered by things like the Unified Energy Interface (UEI). Architecturally, it's also more centralized (a national portal + APIs, government-authority-defined formats via the CEA) than DEG's fully open, protocol-based, "any node can plug in" model.

Bottom line

This is exactly the kind of evidence that answers your earlier question about whether DEG is "just fictional." It's not. A little over a year after the DEG vision paper (Feb 2025), the actual Government of India, through its Ministry of Power, is now writing binding regulatory policy — the National Electricity Data Sharing Framework — that operationalizes DEG's core idea (universal, machine-readable, interoperable energy data) as part of a named national program (India Energy Stack). DEG's data pillar is being turned into real law/policy for one of the largest electricity systems in the world, even though the transaction/energy-flow layers of the full DEG vision are still further out.

Good — the picture is now much clearer, and there's a real government initiative with a name, timeline, and named private-sector partner. Here's the full breakdown.

What India is currently focused on: India Energy Stack (IES)

This is the real, concrete, India-specific execution of DEG-style thinking — and it's moving fast:

- Owner/driver: Ministry of Power, with REC Limited (a state-owned power-sector PSU, formerly Rural Electrification Corporation) acting as the official Nodal Agency.
- Private-sector partner: FSR Global is engaged as the "Knowledge Partner" — this is your answer to "has India contracted a company to develop something": yes, FSR Global is the named private firm helping REC Limited and the Ministry of Power author the IES strategy and architecture documents. (FSR Global even hosts the IES site at ies.fsrglobal.org.)
- Timeline: A task force was launched mid-2025; Strategy/Architecture documents have iterated through v0.1 → v0.2 → v0.3 → v0.4 (released March 2026); the initiative was originally targeted for completion around July 2026.
- What it actually covers: digital identities for energy assets and actors, verifiable credentials, consent-based data sharing, open APIs, shared registries, and "policy-as-code" — i.e., almost exactly DEG's three pillars (identity, machine-readable data, verifiability), formalized into a real government-owned technical architecture.
- The document you already read — the National Electricity Data Sharing Framework 2026 — is literally one regulatory piece of this larger IES effort (the "information flow" layer), currently open for public comment, not yet finalized.
- Real pilots already running:
  - Utility Intelligence Platform (UIP) — a 12-month Proof-of-Concept analytics app built on IES, being piloted with DISCOMs (electricity distribution utilities) in Mumbai, Gujarat, and Delhi.
  - REC Limited demoed a live peer-to-peer energy trading pilot at the India AI Impact Summit 2026 — this is DEG's "transaction flow" vision (villagers/households trading surplus solar directly) shown working, not just described.

There's a second, related but distinct thread: the Unified Energy Interface (UEI) — an open protocol built directly on Beckn Protocol (the same protocol underlying DEG and ONDC), endorsed by the Ministry of Power, focused specifically on EV charging discovery/payment and P2P energy trading. It's driven by a community coalition (UEI Alliance), with real companies already building commercial products on it — Pulse Energy, YoCharge, Kazam Energy. This is narrower and more grassroots/ecosystem-driven than IES, which is the broader, formally government-owned umbrella.

Recap: DEG's vision and goal

DEG (from the FIDE/IEA paper) is a global, non-government, conceptual vision — not owned by any one country. Its goal: propose a universal protocol layer (identity + machine-readable data + cryptographic verifiability) that any country's energy system could adopt, the same way TCP/IP is universal for the internet, so that fragmented energy platforms worldwide (EV charging apps, DER managers, subsidy portals, carbon registries) can interoperate instead of operating in silos. It explicitly names India's UPI/ONDC as the proof that this pattern works, and cites UEI as an early real-world instance — but DEG itself is aspirational and country-agnostic, authored by a non-profit (FIDE) with IEA's endorsement.

The key differentiation

┌───────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                   │               DEG (FIDE/IEA)                │                       India Energy Stack (IES)                        │
├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Scope             │ Global, any country, conceptual             │ India-specific, government-owned                                      │
├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Owner             │ Non-profit (FIDE) + IEA endorsement         │ Ministry of Power, via REC Limited (PSU)                              │
├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Status            │ Vision paper (Feb 2025), no binding         │ Real DPI program, versioned architecture docs, live pilots            │
│                   │ authority                                   │                                                                       │
├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Contracted        │ None — it's a proposal to the world         │ FSR Global engaged as Knowledge Partner; UIP piloted with 3 real      │
│ execution         │                                             │ DISCOMs                                                               │
├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Concrete          │ Illustrative/hypothetical scenarios         │ Real draft regulation (National Electricity Data Sharing Framework),  │
│ deliverables      │ (fictional characters)                      │ real PoC pilots, a live P2P trading demo                              │
├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Timeline          │ No deadline, open-ended global dialogue     │ Targeted completion ~July 2026, taskforce meeting on a cadence        │
└───────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

In short: DEG is the idea; India Energy Stack is India's actual government program turning that idea into a specific, funded, deadline-driven national infrastructure project — with one private company (FSR Global) formally contracted as knowledge partner, a state PSU (REC Limited) as builder/nodal agency, and three real utilities already piloting it. It is, right now, the single most advanced real-world proof that DEG's vision is executable rather than fictional.

❯ so this i where i'm confused too and don't understand what is a real prototype or problem this deg is trying to solve and also what about this IES

Let's slow down and rebuild this from the ground up with one concrete story, no jargon.

The real problem, in one scene

Imagine you install solar panels on your roof in India tomorrow. Here's what actually happens today:

1. You apply for a government subsidy — separate portal, separate paperwork, separate proof of identity/property.
2. You apply for a bank loan to cover t

You (as an individual, not the government) can't build "DEG" or "IES" themselves — those are national-scale infrastructure projects with regulators and utilities involved. What you can build is a small toy version of one narrow slice of the story above, just to see the idea work end-to-end on a tiny scale. For example, a stripped-down version of step 4 above: two fake "apps" — one representing "the homeowner" and one representing "the utility" — that talk to each other through a shared, simple set of rules (a unique ID for the solar system, a standard data format for "this system is verified," and a way to check that verification without a phone call). That's genuinely buildable in an afternoon to a few days, depending on how deep you want to go.

Before I sketch what that would look like technically — what's actually pulling you toward wanting a prototype? Is it:
- You want to personally understand the idea better by building a toy version of it (learning project)?
- You're thinking about a hackathon or demo (like the one FIDE ran in London)?
- You're exploring whether there's a real product/business opportunity here?

That changes how simple or how "real" the prototype should be.

✻ Baked for 30s

❯ i'm exploring whether there's a real product/business opportunity here?

That's a good next question — and worth answering with real market structure in mind, because this ecosystem has a specific economic shape that determines where the money actually is.

The core economic pattern: infra is free, apps on top make money

Both DEG and IES are explicitly designed as open, non-proprietary rails — nobody "owns" them, and nobody charges for using the protocol itself. That's not an accident, it's the same pattern as UPI and ONDC. And in both of those, the protocol layer made no one rich, but the apps built on top of it did:
- UPI is free/public infrastructure → PhonePe, Google Pay, Paytm built billion-dollar businesses on top of it.
- ONDC is a free/open network → Namma Yatri (ride-hailing) and various seller-apps built real businesses on top of it.

So the question isn't "should I build IES/DEG" — you can't, that's literally REC Limited's government mandate. The real question is: what app or service would you build that uses IES/UEI as plumbing once it exists? That's where a founder makes money — same as nobody got rich building HTTP, but plenty got rich building things that run over HTTP.

Where the actual opportunities look real, ranked by how "now" they are

1. Compliance/tooling for DISCOMs and generators (most immediate, least sexy, most fundable)
The National Electricity Data Sharing Framework you read isn't optional busywork — it has real deadlines (metadata catalogue within 12 months, machine-readable public data within 18 months) and every DISCOM, generator, and regulator will need to build or buy a "Data Issuer Portal" that meets the Annexure C design specs (registration, KYC, API publishing, grievance handling, de-identification). Most DISCOMs are not software companies and won't want to build this in-house. That's a straightforward B2G/B2B SaaS play: "compliance-in-a-box" for the National Electricity Data Sharing Framework. This is the closest analog to the account-aggregator tooling companies that emerged around India's fintech data-sharing regs — a real, time-boxed regulatory mandate creating forced demand.

2. Aggregator apps for prosumers/consumers (medium-term, more competitive)
Once UEI-style rails exist for EV charging or P2P trading, someone needs to be the consumer-facing app — the "PhonePe of electricity" — that lets a homeowner see their solar credits, apply for subsidies, join demand-response programs, and trade surplus power, all in one place. Caveat: this lane already has entrants — Pulse Energy, YoCharge, and Kazam Energy are already building UEI-based EV charging apps. Less crowded angles here: P2P trading wallets, or a subsidy/financing discovery app (the "Mihir" scenario from the DEG paper) — nobody's clearly claimed that yet.

3. Demand-response / virtual power plant (VPP) aggregation (higher effort, higher ceiling)
Someone has to aggregate thousands of small batteries/EVs/thermostats and bid them into utility demand-response programs as a pool — this is a genuine operating business (not just an app), requires utility relationships and capital, but has real recurring revenue once running, and directly maps to DEG's "cloud energy storage" example.

4. Analytics/intelligence layer for utilities (speculative, tied to government's own roadmap)
The "Utility Intelligence Platform" being piloted in Mumbai/Gujarat/Delhi is currently being built as part of IES itself — so this lane is riskier: you'd be competing with (or hoping to get subcontracted into) what the government is already building in-house.

My honest read

If you're exploring this as a real business rather than a demo: option 1 (DISCOM compliance tooling) is the strongest starting point — it has a forced deadline, a clear buyer, no existing competitor I found, and doesn't require the UEI/IES transaction layer to even be finished yet (the data-sharing framework is further along than the trading/transaction pieces). The consumer-app plays (2–3) are more exciting but need the underlying network to actually mature first, and part of that lane is already staked out.

Which of these is closer to what you had in mind — or did you have a different angle (e.g., something EV-specific, or something on the finance/subsidy side) you wanted to test?

Two EOIs issued by REC Limited (Ministry of Power's nodal agency) for IES

1. P2P Energy Trading Pilot
- Official title: "IES-Expression of Interest: Join the India Energy Stack (IES) Pilot on Peer to Peer Trading"
- Scope: Build/adapt a trading platform for an inter-state peer-to-peer energy trading network, integrated with utility systems (MDM, RMS, ERP, Billing) via APIs, using consent-based data exchange per IES specs — this is literally DEG's chapter 6.6 example, now a real solicitation.
- Who qualifies: Teams with expertise across power markets, digital platforms, energy systems, and fintech.
- Timeline: EOI due 16 Jan 2026 → onboarding 17 Jan 2026 → final delivery 5 Feb 2026 (a ~3-week sprint — this was run like a hackathon, not a year-long government tender).

2. Regulatory Data Exchange & Analytics Pilot
- Official title: "IES Expression of Interest Regulatory Data Exchange"
- Scope: Dataset packaging, schema validation, provenance tracking, controlled API access in a sandbox — plus explicit encouragement to build "Regulatory GPT-style" applications on top of publicly disclosed regulatory datasets. This is the direct technical cousin of the National Electricity Data Sharing Framework you read — same NEDC/NEDP machine-readable-data thinking, now solicited as a build task.
- Who qualifies: Data validation/quality engineering, regulatory portals/visualization, applied AI/LLM over structured datasets, power-sector regulatory reporting, API/DPI design.
- Timeline: EOI due 25 Feb 2026, 6 PM IST → onboarding 28 Feb 2026 → prototype/demo last week of March 2026.

Important caveats found on the actual pages: participation is entirely voluntaroff, maybe get positioned for future scaling) than a procurement with a guaranteed payout. That P2P trading pilot is what fed directly into the live demo REC showed at the India AI Impact Summit 2026.

What this means for your business exploration

Both windows have technically closed (Jan/Feb 2026, and it's now August), but this tells you something important: REC Limited runs these as recurring open calls, not one-time events — they've already done two in under two months of each other, and the P2P pilot's success (it made it to a live summit demo) suggests more rounds are likely as IES moves from v0.4 toward full rollout. Two takeaways for you:

1. This is the on-ramp, not a competitive tender you missed — companies like the ones already in the UEI Alliance (Pulse Energy, YoCharge, Kazam Energy) got where they are by participating in exactly this kind of early, informal EOI/pilot process, then becoming known ecosystem players.
2. Watching for the next EOI round (likely announced on recindia.nic.in/ies-expression-of-interest or via ies@recindia.com) is a concrete, monitorable action — not something you need to guess at.

Now I can reverse-engineer this precisely — because the P2P pilot actually produced a real winning demo, and I have both EOIs' technical asks in hand.

What the P2P Trading EOI actually asked builders to demonstrate

Not just "build a trading app" — the scope was very specific, and it maps almost exactly to DEG's three pillars in working code:

- Identity management — prove a solar-owning farmer and a buyer in another state can both be uniquely, verifiably identified across DISCOM boundaries.
- Smart meter data exchange — pull real (or simulated) meter data showing actual generation/consumption, not self-reported numbers.
- Interoperable APIs — talk to real utility systems: MDM (meter data management), RMS, ERP, and Billing — via API or secure file interface, not a mocked backend.
- Consent-based data exchange with standard payloads — the seller has to explicitly consent to share their meter data before a trade can happen (this is DEG's "digital consent" concept, literally implemented).
- Price discovery — some mechanism for the two sides to agree on a rate.
- Billing integration and settlement — the trade has to actually resolve into money moving, tied back into the utility's existing billing system, not a separate ledger.
- Auditability — every step traceable/verifiable after the fact.

What actually won and got demoed to the PM

The prototype that made it to the India AI Impact Summit 2026 (and was shown to PM Modi) was: a blockchain-enabled platform where Arun, a farmer in Meerut (UP) with rooftop solar, sold 6 units of surplus solar power directly to a garment shop owner in Delhi, earning ₹30 — executed across state lines, across two different DISCOMs' systems (the pilot involved BRPL, TPDDL, and PVVNL, serving ~1.25 crore consumers combined).

Notice what's actually impressive there isn't the transaction size (₹30!) — it's that it proved the hard, boring plumbing problem: two strangers, in two different states, under two different utilities, could transa

What the Regulatory Data Exchange EOI asked for

Different shape entirely — this one wanted tooling, not a consumer app:
- Dataset packaging, schema validation, provenance tracking (i.e., can you prove this dataset wasn't tampered with and trace where it came from).
- Controlled API access inside a sandbox environment.
- Explicit encouragement toward "Regulatory GPT-style" applications — i.e., an AI assistant that lets a regulator, journalist, or researcher query messy government electricity data in plain English and get a trustworthy answer.

The pattern, if you're sizing up a future round

Both EOIs share a formula worth noting: they didn't want a business pitch or a slide deck — they wanted a working, narrow, end-to-end technical proof of one specific interoperability claim, built and demoed in 2–4 weeks, using REC/FSR's published IES specs and sandbox rather than inventing your own architecture. The judging bar wasn't "is this a good business" — it was "does this actually prove the plumbing works across a real boundary (two states, two DISCOMs, two data silos)." So a competitive submission for the next round would look like: pick one narrow, real friction point (e.g., a subsidy-to-billing handoff, an EV charging cross-DISCOM payment, a demand-response settlement), build the smallest possible working integration against the IES sandbox/spec, and demo it with a real (even if tiny) transaction — not a mockup.

So 6 of 8 are genuinely still unclaimed. Of those, three stand out as realistically buildable by you right now, without needing real DISCOM system access:

My recommendation: Consumer Energy Passport

This is the cleanest possible "hello world" of the entire IES concept, for a specific reason: it only needs three actors (an issuer, a holder, a verifier) instead of a two-sided marketplace, and it directly exercises the two building blocks — Identity and Credentials — that the spec calls "core dependencies in almost every use case." The narrative is dead simple to demo: "A consumer moves to a new city, shares one digital credential with their new DISCOM, and skips repeated KYC." It's the same pattern as DigiLocker (which Indian evaluators already trust and understand), applied to energy identity.

What the prototype would concretely be:
1. Issuer service (stands in for a DISCOM) — takes a consumer's connection/meter/asset data and issues a signed, tamper-evident digital credential (a verifiable credential / signed JSON object, per the spec's "PROOF" building block).
2. Wallet (stands in for the consumer) — holds that credential, can present it as a QR code or shareable link, consent-gated.
3. Verifier service (stands in for a new DISCOM, a bank, or a solar installer) — scans/receives the credential and cryptographically verifies it's authentic and unaltered, without ever calling the original issuer.

That's a genuinely finishable prototype — a backend issuer API, a signing/verification library, and a minimal wallet UI — buildable in days, not months, and it maps directly onto vocabulary the spec itself uses (Identity, Registries, Credentials), which matters a lot for how credible your EOI submission reads to REC/FSR.

Strong alternate if you'd rather avoid needing real government data at all: Tariff Intelligence. This one converts published tariff-order PDFs into the spec's "Policy Pack" format — machine-readable, computable tariff rules already public documents), and it plays directly to what I can actually help you build well (structured extraction from real PDFs into a verifiable schema).

My Energy Digest is a close third — same credential pattern as Passport, but focused on usage-history proof for a green loan, per the spec's own "bank" example — good if you want to lean into the fintech-adjacent story.

Which of these feels right to commit to — Consumer Energy Passport, Tariff Intelligence, or My Energy Digest — or do you want me to lay out all three architectures side by side before you pick?

The big unlock: what a TSP actually has to build is tiny

The full IES architecture is: Register (W3C did:web identity) → Discover (Beckn Protocol, via a pre-built open-source adapter called Beckn ONIX) → Exchange (standard JSON-LD schemas + W3C Verifiable Credentials, held in DigiLocker). The critical finding: you don't build the protocol layer — that's already built. The only thing a real TSP submission actually codes is what they call a "Part-2 mapping" — roughly 200–1,000 lines of code translating a client's internal systems (billing, CIS, meter data, DERMS) into the IES schema shapes. Everything else is pre-built infrastructure you configure, not software you write from scratch.

There's also a working public devkit right now: git clone github.com/beckn/DEG → docker compose up gives you a local sandbox with Postman collections for exactly the use cases we've been discussing. You could have a sandbox exchange running locally within an hour.

What's already claimed vs. genuinely still open

This is the most important strategic finding. Five use cases are done — fully specified, built, and already demoed by four real DISCOMs (PVVNL in UP, APEPDCL in Andhra Pradesh, DGVCL in Gujarat, Tata Power in Maharashtra) in a 30-day "DISCOM Challenge" that ran 21 May–21 June 2026: P2P Trading, Consumer Energy Passport, Consumer Meter Digest, Smart Meter Data Exchange, and DER Visibility. Building any of those now means competing against a finished, government-validated reference implementation.

Only two use cases remain genuinely open and unproven: UC2 (Regulatory Data Exchange & Analytics) and UC3 (Policy as Code / Tariff Intelligence) — both explicitly marked "Work in Progress" in the actual schema docs, with named unresolved gaps:
- UC2's ArrFiling v0.5 schema has open questions around cost-category taxonomy convergence.
- UC3's IES_Policy schema (with energySlabs[]/surchargeTariffs[]) has open questions around workbook-attachment conventions and amendment/versioning rules.

This directly validates the "Tariff Intelligence" direction I recommended earlier — UC3 maps to it precisely, and it's confirmed as one of only two genuinely unsolved use cases.

The catch: both EOIs already closed, and there's no guaranteed payoff

Both UC2 and UC3 EOIs had a submission deadline of 25 Feb 2026 — already passed. And REC's own language is blunt: they reserve the right to accept/reject "without assigning any reason," acceptance "does not guarantee future deployment," and no compensation is mentioned anywhere for EOI participation. This isn't a paid government contract — it's an unpaid door-opener into the ecosystem.

The real path forward, given the EOI window is shut

There's a second, deadline-free channel: "Contribute to IES" (ies.recindia.org.in/docs/propose-a-schema) — an ongoing governance process (not EOI-bound) where anyone — DISCOMs, regulators, tech vendors — can propose fixes to exactly the kind of open schema gaps UC2/UC3 still have, reviewed through a 6-step process (acknowledgement → central IES team review → community consultation → publication → ecosystem adoption). No deadline, open right now.

So the concrete move: build a working Part-2 mapping prototype for UC3 (Tariff Intelligence) against the local devkit — using real public tariff-order PDFs, since that needs no DISCOM access — that specifically resolves one of the named open gaps (e.g., the workbook-attachment convention problem), and submit it through the Contribute channel rather than waiting for the next EOI round. That gets you real visibility with the IES technical team now, and positions you to move fast (past EOI sprints were only ~3–4 weeks) when the next formal EOI round opens.

One more governance detail worth knowing: a formal "IES Cell" is being constituted under the Central Electricity Authority (CEA) to own specifications going forward — this is more statutory/permanent than the "REC as informal nodal agency" picture we had before, and is worth watching as the real long-term counterparty.

Want me to pull up the actual UC3 IES_Policy schema and the devkit's Postman collection next, so we can scope exactly what a first working prototype would need to do?

The actual UC3 schema — IES_Policy

This is the real, live example from the docs (a Mumbai residential tariff):
{
  "@type": "IES_Policy",
  "id": "policy-mumbai-res-001",
  "policyID": "MUM-RES-T1",
  "policyName": "Mumbai Residential Telescopic 2026",
  "policyType": "TARIFF",
  "programID": "program-merashehar-001",
  "samplingInterval": "R/2026-04-10T00:00:00Z/P1M",
  "energySlabs": [
    { "id": "slab-0-100",    "start": 0,   "end": 100,  "price": 4.5 },
    { "id": "slab-301-plus", "start": 301, "end": null, "price": 9.8 }
  ],
  "surchargeTariffs": [
    { "id": "surcharge-evening-peak", "recurrence": "P1D",
      "interval": { "start": "T18:00:00Z", "duration": "PT4H" }, "value": 20, "unit": "PERCENT" }
  ]
}

Roles: the SERC (regulator) is the BPP (publisher, signs the policy with its did:web key); a DISCOM's billing system, a consumer app, or a smart meter is the BAP (consumer, ingests the same object and evaluates a bill locally). Publication is public by default (accessMethod: INLINE, settlement 0) — no contract needed to read it.

Status, in their own words: "🚧 Work in progress... schema currently lives upstream at beckn/DEG (ies-specs branch), migrating to a first-class schemas/Tariff/v0.x/." Four things are explicitly still unresolved (this is your opening): no issuer/signature field defined yet in the schema itself, no formal amendment/replaces convention, no currency field on energySlabs[].price, and policy types beyond TARIFF/DISPATCH_GUIDE (deviation penalties, DR rules, data-quality SLAs) are still undefined.

This spins up a local sandbox — two ONIX adapters (onix-bap, onix-bpp), stand-in apps, a router, Redis — pre-wired with placeholder identities. There's a matching example set specifically for this use case at devkits/data-exchange/uc3-tariff-policy/examples (Mumbai residential + a Karnataka LT2 industrial time-of-day example), plus a Postman collection (uc3-tariff-policy) to fire real requests at it.

└────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────┘

Steps 1–7 are fully buildable by you right now, with zero government cooperation needed, using only public tariff orders.

What this means for the prototype

The minimum viable, demo-able build is exactly two pieces, both directly targeting the schema's own open gaps:
1. A converter: take a real SERC tariff order PDF (e.g. Mumbai residential, or the Karnataka LT2 example already in the devkit) → parse it → emit valid IES_Policy JSON-LD → sign it with a test did:web key.
2. A billing evaluator: consume that same signed object → given a usage number and a timestamp → find the matching slab, find the matching time-of-day surcharge, compute the bill → show two independently-written evaluators produce identical numbers from the same policy object (this is literally the point the whole use case exists to prove).

Bonus, since the schema explicitly lacks a currency field and an amendment/replaces convention: propose a fix for one of those as part of the submission — that's a genuine, named, unresolved gap you'd be helping close, not busywork.

// Minimal deterministic JSON canonicalization (subset of RFC 8785 JCS):
// recursively sort object keys, no insignificant whitespace. Sufficient for our
// flat numeric/string/array shapes. The Python evaluator implements the same
// rule (see evaluator.py:canonicalize) so both sides produce byte-identical
// bytes-to-sign / bytes-to-hash from the same object.
export function canonicalize(value) {
  if (value === null || typeof value !== "object") {
    return JSON.stringify(value);
  }

// over the canonicalized policy object. Never trusts the envelope's `issuer`
// field blindly — the key used to verify comes from the DID document, not from
// the file being checked.
import { createPublicKey, verify } from "node:crypto";
import { createPublicKey, verify as cryptoVerify } from "node:crypto";
import { readFileSync } from "node:fs";
import { canonicalize } from "./canonicalize.js";

const POLICIES_DIR = new URL("../policies/", import.meta.url);
const KEYS_DIR = new URL("../keys/", import.meta.url);

const file = process.argv[2] ?? "mumbai-res-tariff.signed.json";
const envelope = JSON.parse(readFileSync(new URL(file, POLICIES_DIR), "utf-8"));

// Real flow: fetch https://<issuer-domain>/.well-known/did.json.
// Prototype flow: read the locally-generated did.json standing in for that fetch.
const did = JSON.parse(readFileSync(new URL("did.json", KEYS_DIR), "utf-8"));
if (did.id !== envelope.publication.issuer) {
  console.error(`FAIL: envelope claims issuer ${envelope.publication.issuer}, resolved DID is ${did.id}`);
  process.exit(1);
export function verifyEnvelope(envelope, didDocument) {
  if (didDocument.id !== envelope.publication.issuer) {
    return { valid: false, reason: `envelope claims issuer ${envelope.publication.issuer}, resolved DID is ${didDocument.id}` }
;
  }
  const vm = didDocument.verificationMethod.find(
    (v) => v.id === envelope.publication.proof.verificationMethod
  );
  if (!vm) {
    return { valid: false, reason: "verificationMethod not found in issuer's DID document" };
  }
  const publicKey = createPublicKey({ key: vm.publicKeyJwk, format: "jwk" });
  const canonicalBytes = Buffer.from(canonicalize(envelope.policy), "utf-8");
  const signature = Buffer.from(envelope.publication.proof.signatureValue, "base64");
  const valid = cryptoVerify(null, canonicalBytes, publicKey, signature);
  return valid ? { valid: true } : { valid: false, reason: "signature does not match resolved issuer key" };
}

const vm = did.verificationMethod.find(
  (v) => v.id === envelope.publication.proof.verificationMethod
);
if (!vm) {
  console.error("FAIL: verificationMethod not found in issuer's DID document");
  process.exit(1);
// CLI entry point: `node src/verifyPolicy.js [file.json]`
if (import.meta.url === `file://${process.argv[1]}`) {
  const POLICIES_DIR = new URL("../policies/", import.meta.url);
  const KEYS_DIR = new URL("../keys/", import.meta.url);
  const file = process.argv[2] ?? "mumbai-res-tariff.signed.json";
  const envelope = JSON.parse(readFileSync(new URL(file, POLICIES_DIR), "utf-8"));
  const did = JSON.parse(readFileSync(new URL("did.json", KEYS_DIR), "utf-8"));
  const result = verifyEnvelope(envelope, did);
  if (result.valid) {
    console.log(`PASS  ${envelope.policy.policyID} (${envelope.policy.id}) — signature verified against ${did.id}`);
  } else {
    console.error(`FAIL  ${envelope.policy.policyID} — ${result.reason}`);
    process.exit(1);
  }
}

const publicKey = createPublicKey({ key: vm.publicKeyJwk, format: "jwk" });
const canonicalBytes = Buffer.from(canonicalize(envelope.policy), "utf-8");
const signature = Buffer.from(envelope.publication.proof.signatureValue, "base64");

const valid = verify(null, canonicalBytes, publicKey, signature);

if (valid) {
  console.log(`PASS  ${envelope.policy.policyID} (${envelope.policy.id}) — signature verified against ${did.id}`);
} else {
  console.error(`FAIL  ${envelope.policy.policyID} — signature does NOT match resolved issuer key`);
  process.exit(1);
}

What actually exists now:

That's now written up in proposed-extensions.md alongside the three gaps the docs already named (no issuer/proof field, no currency field, no amendment convention), each with a proposed backward-compatible fix.

## Live sandbox round trip — actually done, not simulated

Docker wasn't running in this environment, so the prototype stops at "produces
and consumes the correct signed payload" rather than "exchanges it over a live
Beckn round-trip." To close that gap:
The real `beckn/DEG` devkit is cloned at `../ies-devkit` and its
`data-exchange` sandbox stack (onix-bap, onix-bpp, beckn-router, redis,
sandbox-bap, sandbox-bpp — 7 containers) was brought up in Docker and proven
end to end:

```bash
git clone https://github.com/beckn/DEG.git    # already done -> ../ies-devkit
cd ../ies-devkit/devkits/data-exchange/install
docker compose up -d

Then replace the sandbox's stand-in BPP app with a small webhook service that:
1. On `discover`, returns the catalogue shape in
   `uc3-tariff-policy/examples/publish-catalog.json` (already real, from the
   devkit).
2. On `confirm`, calls `authorPolicy.js`'s output and returns it as the
   `on_confirm` → `on_status` `dataPayload`, matching
   `examples/on-status-response-ready-inline.json` exactly.
3. On the BAP side, calls `evaluator.js`'s `computeBill()` on receipt.
**Setup — inject our real signed policy as the BPP's response.** The
sandbox's stand-in BPP app (`sandbox-bpp`) serves canned JSON fixtures per
action. Its default `on_status.json` fixture was replaced with the real
example shape from `uc3-tariff-policy/examples/on-status-response-ready-inline.json`,
with the `dataPayload` swapped for **our own freshly authored + signed policy**
(`policies/mumbai-res-tariff.signed.json`'s `.policy` field) — the original
generic fixture is preserved as `on_status.json.original`.

That turns this from "two scripts that agree" into "a real Beckn network
round-trip that agrees" — the actual conformance bar described in the docs
(`ies-docs-technical-summary.md`, "Conformance" section).
**Round trip, run via `./live-sandbox-roundtrip.sh`:**

1. **`discover`** — sent as a broadcast (no `bppId` filter, per the real
   example payload) against the actual live `nfh.global/testnet-deg` DeDi
   registry. Result: **it reached a real, currently-registered external
   participant** — `IntelliGrid AMI Services` (`bppId: fabric.nfh.global`,
   a live public endpoint at `https://34.93.165.42.sslip.io/beckn/`) — and
   returned a real AMI meter-data catalogue. This wasn't expected going in;
   it's direct proof the IES testnet is genuinely live right now, not just a
   local fixture.
   webhook → `on_confirm` callback → back through the router → `onix-bap` →
   `sandbox-bap` webhook. **Schema validation passed at every hop** (see
   `onix-bpp`/`onix-bap` debug logs).
3. **`status`** — same path, this time delivering our injected policy.
   `onix-bap`'s own validator logged:
   **`Validation passed for @type: IES_Policy at path: message.contract.performance[0].performanceAttributes.dataPayload`**
   — i.e. the real ONIX schema validator (not our own code) independently
   confirmed our authored object conforms to the upstream `IES_Policy` schema.

**Closing the loop.** The payload `sandbox-bap` actually received (logged to
stdout, extracted from `docker logs sandbox-bap`) was pulled out and fed into
both evaluators exactly as delivered — not the locally-authored copy:

```bash
node -e '/* see live-sandbox-roundtrip.sh output for the extraction one-liner */'
python evaluator.py received-from-sandbox.json test/fixtures/usage-normal.json
```

Both produced **Rs2428.98** — identical to the standalone test result,
now proven against a payload that actually traveled through the real signed
Beckn message path, not just through our own scripts.

**What this does not yet prove:** we injected our policy into `sandbox-bpp`'s
fixture rather than standing up our own real BPP webhook service — the
signing/routing/validation are all genuinely onix's, but the "business logic"
that decided *what* to serve is still a swapped-in file, not live code. The
honest next step to reach full conformance is replacing `sandbox-bpp` with a
small real webhook service that calls `authorPolicy.js` directly.

● Web Search("India Energy Stack V2G vehicle-to-grid EOI 2026 REC Limited")
Did 1 search in 8s

  Searched for 2 patterns, ran 1 shell command

I checked both the official spec and the devkit repo directly — V2G is a real gap, but a much rawer one than UC3. Here's exactly where it stands.

Short answer: no, there's no EOI or defined use case for V2G yet

V2G isn't one of the 8 official IES sandbox use cases in the v0.4 Strategy Document, and it isn't one of the two EOIs REC Limited has actually run (P2P trading, regulatory data exchange), nor one of the two still-open "work in progress" ones we targeted (UC2 regulatory filing, UC3 policy-as-code). There's no V2G schema, no example payloads, and no devkit built around it.

Where V2G actually shows up in the real specs

I grepped the whole cloned repo and the docs. It appears in exactly two places, both as metadata tags, not a working flow:

1. EV_V2G — a sub-type value inside the DER/asset registry schema (ElectricityCredential), alongside EV_CHARGER, SOLAR_PV, BESS, etc. This just lets a charger declare "I am this kind of device" during registration (DER Visibility / Consumer Energy Passport) — same as tagging a solar panel's type.
2. V2G_ENABLED — a capability flag in the real EV-charging implementation guide (docs/implementation-guides/v2/EV_Charging.md), described as: "Charger/site supports bidirectional power transfer (V2G), e.g., per ISO 15118-20 implementations."

So the stack currently lets a station say "I can do V2G" — but there's no defined message flow for actually discovering V2G-capable chargers, negotiating a discharge session, metering the power sent back to the grid, or settling payment for it. The discover→confirm→session lifecycle that's fully built out for EV charging (devkits/ev-charging/uc1-ev-charging — 20 real example payloads, discovery through rating) simply doesn't have a discharge-direction counterpart yet.

There's a real V2G project in India — but it's a different one entirely

Worth being precise here so you don't conflate two separate things: the India Smart Grid Forum (ISGF), a public-private advisory body to the Ministry of Power, ran a real physical V2G pilot — four Tata Nexon EVs retrofitted with bidirectional AC chargers, demonstrated exporting power back to the grid at sites in Delhi and Kerala, using AC-based bidirectional charging (cheaper than the DC approach most global V2G pilots use). That's a genuine, hardware-level demonstration — but it's not connected to REC Limited, FSR Global, or the IES/Beckn digital-protocol effort we've been tracking. It's proving the electrical engineering works; nobody has yet defined the digital protocol layer (identity, discovery, settlement) for it the way UC3 defined one for tariffs.

What this means for you

This is a genuinely bigger, rawer opportunity than UC3 — but also a materially riskier one:

- Upside: it's completely unclaimed. If REC/FSR ever do open a V2G use case, you'd be building from a blank page, not competing with anything.
- Downside: there's no schema to build against, no example payloads, no signal from the government that this is coming next, and real bidirectional-charging hardware costs 2–3x a normal charger — meaning any real pilot needs actual EVs and chargers, not just software, unlike the tariff use case which needed nothing but public PDFs.

Given that, V2G today is closer to "watch for it" than "build a prototype for it" — there's no spec to conform to yet, so anything we built would be pure speculation rather than the kind of "matches their real schema" proof point that made the UC3 work credible. Want me to keep an eye on whether REC/FSR add V2G as a named use case in a future strategy version, or would you rather explore a different angle in the meantime?

// Authors a real MERC (Maharashtra Electricity Regulatory Commission) residential
// tariff as a schema-conformant IES_Policy object, then wraps it in a
// "publication envelope" and signs it.
//
// The IES_Policy fields below match, field-for-field, the real example shipped in
// the official devkit at:
//   devkits/data-exchange/uc3-tariff-policy/examples/on-status-response-ready-inline.json
// (cloned from github.com/beckn/DEG) — i.e. this is the tariff the IES team itself
// uses to describe the use case, derived from MERC Order Case No. 226 of 2023.
//
// The docs are explicit that IES_Policy currently has NO issuer/proof field and
// NO amendment/replaces field (see ies-docs-raw/page_016.md, Section 8.4 "Fields
// Not Present in the Current Upstream Policy Object"). Rather than smuggling an
// ungoverned `proof` field into the core object (which the docs explicitly warn
// against), this authors a separate PUBLICATION ENVELOPE that carries exactly
// those three still-open items as a proposed, backward-compatible convention:
//   - issuer (signer identity)
//   - proof (detached signature over the canonical policy)
//   - replaces (prior policy id, for amendments) + currency (missing on EnergySlab)
// See ../proposed-extensions.md for the write-up of why this shape, not a
// core-schema change, is the safe way to close those gaps.
import { sign } from "node:crypto";
// CLI: authors + signs the real MERC-derived tariff policy and writes it to
// disk. Uses policyEngine.js — the exact same code path the live webhook
// server (webhook-server/server.js) runs on every `status` request.
import { readFileSync, writeFileSync } from "node:fs";
import { canonicalize } from "./canonicalize.js";
import { buildSignedPolicy } from "./policyEngine.js";

const POLICIES_DIR = new URL("../policies/", import.meta.url);
const KEYS_DIR = new URL("../keys/", import.meta.url);

// --- 1. The IES_Policy object itself (schema-conformant, no extra fields) ---
const policy = {
  "@context": "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/beckn/DEG/ies-specs/specification/external/schema/ies/core/context.jsonld",
  "@type": "IES_Policy",
  id: "policy-mumbai-res-001",
  objectType: "POLICY",
  createdDateTime: "2024-04-10T11:00:00Z",
  modificationDateTime: "2024-04-10T11:00:00Z",
  programID: "program-merashehar-001",
  policyID: "MUM-RES-T1",
  policyName: "Mumbai Residential Telescopic 2024",
  policyType: "TARIFF",
  samplingInterval: "R/2024-04-10T00:00:00Z/P1M",
  energySlabs: [
    { id: "slab-0-100", "@type": "EnergySlab", start: 0, end: 100, price: 4.5 },
    { id: "slab-101-300", "@type": "EnergySlab", start: 101, end: 300, price: 7.2 },
    { id: "slab-301-plus", "@type": "EnergySlab", start: 301, end: null, price: 9.8 }
  ],
  surchargeTariffs: [
    {
      id: "surcharge-evening-peak",
      "@type": "SurchargeTariff",
      recurrence: "P1D",
      interval: { start: "T18:00:00Z", duration: "PT4H" },
      value: 20,
      unit: "PERCENT"
    },
    {
      id: "discount-night-offpeak",
      "@type": "SurchargeTariff",
      recurrence: "P1D",
      interval: { start: "T23:00:00Z", duration: "PT6H" },
      value: -10,
      unit: "PERCENT"
    }
  ]
};

// --- 2. Sign the canonical policy bytes with the publisher's Ed25519 key ---
const privateKeyPem = readFileSync(new URL("private.pem", KEYS_DIR));
const canonicalBytes = Buffer.from(canonicalize(policy), "utf-8");
const signature = sign(null, canonicalBytes, privateKeyPem); // Ed25519: no digest algo needed
const publicationEnvelope = buildSignedPolicy(privateKeyPem, "did:web:ies.merc.example");

// --- 3. Wrap in the proposed publication envelope ---
const publicationEnvelope = {
  policy,
  publication: {
    issuer: "did:web:ies.merc.example",
    issuedAt: new Date().toISOString(),
    replaces: null, // set to a prior `id` on amendment, per proposed-extensions.md
    currency: "INR", // policy-level currency, since EnergySlab.price has none upstream
    proof: {
      type: "Ed25519Signature2020",
      verificationMethod: "did:web:ies.merc.example#key-1",
      created: new Date().toISOString(),
      proofPurpose: "assertionMethod",
      // signs the canonicalized `policy` object only — the envelope's own
      // metadata (issuer/issuedAt/replaces/currency) is NOT covered, by design,
      // so re-wrapping an unchanged policy for redistribution never invalidates it
      signatureValue: signature.toString("base64")
    }
  }
};

writeFileSync(
  new URL("mumbai-res-tariff.signed.json", POLICIES_DIR),
  JSON.stringify(publicationEnvelope, null, 2)
);

console.log("Authored + signed: policies/mumbai-res-tariff.signed.json");
console.log("  policyID:", policy.policyID, " id:", policy.id);
console.log("  policyID:", publicationEnvelope.policy.policyID, " id:", publicationEnvelope.policy.id);
console.log("  issuer:  ", publicationEnvelope.publication.issuer);

const ONIX_BPP_CALLER = "http://onix-bpp:8082/bpp/caller";

// onix-bpp's receiver forwards the inbound request to us and expects a quick
// ACK back — the actual on_x reply is a SEPARATE outbound call WE make into
// onix-bpp's own /bpp/caller/ endpoint, which signs it and relays it onward
// to the BAP over the router. (Confirmed by comparing against how the
// original fixture-based sandbox-bpp behaved: it never returned the on_x
// body as the webhook's HTTP response either — it must have made this same
// second call, which is why the round trip worked before we could see this.)
async function emitOnAction(action, payload) {
  const url = `${ONIX_BPP_CALLER}/${action}`;
  const res = await fetch(url, {
    method: "POST",
    headers: { "Content-Type": "application/jso
  console.log(`[emit] POST ${url} -> HTTP ${res.status}: ${text.slice(0, 200)}`);
  if (!res.ok) throw new Error(`onix-bpp caller rejected ${action}: HTTP ${res.status} ${text}`);
}

const server = createServer(async (req, res) => {
  if (req.method === "GET" && req.url === "/api/health") {
    res.writeHead(200, { "Content-Type": "application/json" });

    const action = match[1];
    try {
      const body = await readBody(req);
      let response;
      if (action === "confirm") response = handleConfirm(body);
      else if (action === "status") response = handleStatus(body);
      else response = genericAck(body, action);

      // ACK the inbound webhook call immediately.
      res.writeHead(200, { "Content-Type": "application/json" });
      res.end(JSON.stringify(response));
      res.end(JSON.stringify({ message: { ack: { status: "ACK" } } }));

      // Then build the real reply and push it back through onix-bpp's own
      // caller interface, which signs and rela
        await emitOnAction("on_status", handleStatus(body));
      }
      // other actions (select, init, track, ...): ACK only, no reply needed
      // for the UC3 flow this prototype exercises.
    } catch (err) {
      console.error(`Error handling ${action}:`, err);
      res.writeHead(500, { "Content-Type": "application/json" });
      res.end(JSON.stringify({ error: String(err) }));
      // response headers already sent (ACK) — log only, matches how a real
      // async webhook backend can't retroactively fail the original call.
    }
    return;
  }

function genericAck(body, action) {
  const contractId = body?.message?.contract?.id ?? "770e9500-f30c-52e5-b827-557766551301";
  return {
    context: baseContext(body?.context, `on_${action}`),
    message: { contract: { id: contractId, status: { code: "ACTIVE" } } }
  };
}

## Live sandbox round trip — a real webhook backend, not a swapped-in file

The real `beckn/DEG` devkit is cloned at `../ies-devkit` and its
`data-exchange` sandbox stack (onix-bap, onix-bpp, beckn-router, redis,
sandbox-bap, sandbox-bpp — 7 containers) was brought up in Docker and proven
end to end:
The real `beckn/DEG` devkit is cloned at `../ies-devkit`. Its `data-exchange`
sandbox stack (onix-bap, onix-bpp, beckn-router, redis, sandbox-bap,
sandbox-bpp — 7 containers) runs in Docker, and **the `sandbox-bpp` service
has been replaced with our own code** (`webhook-server/`, built by
`docker-compose.yml`'s `build:` block for that service) — not a static
fixture file. Bring it up with:

```bash
cd ../ies-devkit/devkits/data-exchange/install
docker compose up -d
docker compose up -d --build
```

**Setup — inject our real signed policy as the BPP's response.** The
sandbox's stand-in BPP app (`sandbox-bpp`) serves canned JSON fixtures per
action. Its default `on_status.json` fixture was replaced with the real
example shape from `uc3-tariff-policy/examples/on-status-response-ready-inline.json`,
with the `dataPayload` swapped for **our own freshly authored + signed policy**
(`policies/mumbai-res-tariff.signed.json`'s `.policy` field) — the original
generic fixture is preserved as `on_status.json.original`.
**How the BPP side actually works now.** onix-bpp's routing config
(`config/local-simple-routing-BPPReceiver.yaml`) forwards every incoming
action to `http://sandbox-bpp:3002/api/webhook/<action>` — that hostname now
resolves to `webhook-server/server.js`. On a `status` call, it calls
`buildSignedPolicy()` from `src/policyEngine.js` — the exact function
`npm run author` uses — fresh, live, per request, then pushes the result back
into onix-bpp's own outbound interface (`POST http://onix-bpp:8082/bpp/caller/on_status`),
which signs and relays it onward. (Getting this right took a few iterations —
onix's real validator rejected our first two attempts for missing fields
[`commitments[].resources`, then `schema:temporalCoverage`] that weren't
obvious from the docs alone; the real example payload was the source of
truth for the fix.)

**Round trip, run via `./live-sandbox-roundtrip.sh`:**

   local fixture.

❯ great now first i need to look back from start what we did and what was the problem and did the above we did is useful for the government and what problem did we solved. so is that the government was not able to do this and we did it and so on many confusions arises as i have no idea

Totally fair to want this laid out plainly — let's clear this up properly, because there's a real misconception buried in "did government fail and we succeeded," and it's worth fixing directly.

The problem, one more time, simply

India's electricity system runs on real infrastructure (grids, meters, tariffs) but the paperwork connecting all of it — subsidy approvals, tariff orders, billing data — is fragmented across disconnected offices, mostly still PDFs and manual re-typing. The government is building a shared digital rulebook (India Energy Stack) so all these systems can talk to each other automatically instead of through humans re-entering data.

What we actually built

We picked one small, specific, still-unfinished corner of that rulebook — how a regulator's tariff order (electricity pricing rules) gets published as data instead of a PDF — and built a working proof that it can be done: a real tariff, signed, served live, checked by the government's own validation software, and correctly computed into a bill by two independent programs.

Now, the actual question: did the government fail, and did we succeed where they couldn't?

No — and this is the important correction. Here's what actually happened, precisely:

The government's own technical documentation lists 8 specific sub-problems this stack needs to solve. They didn't fail to solve all 8 — they solved 5 of them already, with real utilities, in a real pilot, months ago. For the remaining ones, they did something completely normal for this kind of project: they published the unsolved parts on purpose and put out an open invitation for outside people to attempt them. That's not a failure — it's how they intend to build this. Big infrastructure projects (this is the same playbook UPI and ONDC used) deliberately leave pieces open so a wider pool of companies and developers can contribute pieces, rather than one government team trying to build everything alone.

So the honest framing is: the government named an open problem on purpose, and we're one of potentially many people who could attempt an answer to it — not "they couldn't do it and we did." They almost certainly have their own team also working on this exact piece in parallel. Nothing about our prototype means the government was stuck.

So is what we built actually useful?

Yes — but specifically, not universally. Here's the honest scope of that usefulness:

- It's a real, working demonstration that their proposed approach for tariffs is technically buildable — which is genuinely useful information for the team writing the spec, since "does this actually work when someone builds it" is exactly what an early-stage spec needs tested.
- We found a real gap they hadn't documented: their own rulebook doesn't specify how a time-of-day price adjustment interacts with multi-tier pricing, which means two "correct" implementations could compute different bills. That's a legitimate, concrete, actionable finding — the kind of thing spec authors actually want to hear, because it's the sort of ambiguity that causes real bugs later if nobody catches it early.
- It is not: reviewed, accepted, adopted, or seen by anyone at the Ministry of Power yet. It's a submission we're about to make through their normal public feedback channel (Contribute-to-IES) — the same channel any company, student, or engineer can use. It carries no special weight until real people there look at it and decide whether it's useful.

The plain-language bottom line

We didn't rescue a broken government project. We took one deliberately-left-open piece of a real, large, working national technology program, built a small, honestly-tested proof that a specific idea works, and are packaging it to hand to the team responsible — through the exact public door they built for that purpose. That's a legitimate, useful contribution at the scale of "one solid technical data point," not "solving the problem the government couldn't."

Does that resolve the confusion, or is there a specific part — the EOI process, the schema gap we found, or how the government reviews these submissions — you want me to go deeper on?

Good that you asked this directly — I want to correct the premise here, because the evidence actually points the other way.

We almost certainly are NOT the first to attempt this

There was already a formal, official EOI specifically for this exact use case — UC3, Policy as Code — that REC Limited ran back in January–February 2026, with prototypes due for a demo by the last week of March 2026. That's roughly five months before we started. The EOI explicitly said: "Selected TSPs will be required to build or adapt policy-pack tooling." That means real companies were invited, and very likely did submit attempts, months before we touched this.

I just searched for what came of that round, specifically, and here's what I found — and didn't find:

- No public announcement, press release, or news coverage of a completed UC3/Tariff-Intelligence demo — which is a real contrast, because the other use case (P2P trading) got a splashy public demo shown to the Prime Minister at the AI Impact Summit. If UC3 had produced something similarly finished, I'd expect to see at least some coverage of it, and I found none.
- The official technical documentation — dated within days of our own research — still marks Policy as Code as "Work in Progress," with the schema still sitting in an upstream draft branch rather than promoted to the finalized schema folder. That's the strongest piece of evidence: whatever happened in that earlier EOI round, nothing from it has been accepted into the canonical spec as of right now.

So what's the honest picture?

We can't claim to be first, and I shouldn't have let that framing stand unchallenged — it's quite likely other teams already tried this back in February–March. What we can honestly claim: whatever anyone else submitted, it hasn't been finalized, adopted, or made canonical — the problem is still officially open today. Our attempt is independent, freshly built, and stands on its own merits regardless of who else has tried.

This actually matters for how you should think about our submission: the Contribute-to-IES channel isn't a single winner-take-all contest — it's designed to take in multiple community inputs and let the secretariat weigh them. Being "not first" doesn't make a genuinely working, independently-validated submission worthless; it just means we should submit it as one credible input among possibly several, not as "the" solution nobody else found.

Let's slow all the way down and rebuild this properly — you're right to want the ground truth before accepting any of it. I'll draw a clear line between "what already existed before we touched anything" and "what we actually built," because that distinction is exactly what's missing right now.

Part 1: What already existed — the repo, before we touched it

When we started building, we didn't write code from a blank page. We ran one command — git clone https://github.com/beckn/DEG.git — which downloaded a folder of code and documents that the India Energy Stack team had already published publicly, months before this conversation started. We didn't make any of this. Here's who did and what it contains:

Who built it: The same people we identified earlier — REC Limited (the government-designated builder), FSR Global (their hired technical partner), coordinated through the "India-Energy-Stack" and "Beckn" open-source organizations on GitHub. This is their official public toolkit for anyone who wants to build software that plugs into India Energy Stack.

What was inside it, in plain terms — three kinds of things:
1. Written rulebooks (specifications): documents describing, in words, what each "use case" is supposed to do — e.g., "a regulator publishes a tariff order as data instead of a PDF."
2. Data shape definitions (schemas): think of these like a blank form with labeled boxes — "put the price here, put the start date here" — so that any two different programs filling out the same form produce data that means the same thing.
3. A "practice network" (the devkit/sandbox): a small, safe, toy copy of their real government server software that you can run on your own laptop to test against, without needing to be a real utility or regulator.

None of that — the rulebook, the form, or the practice servers — is something we invented. It was already there, sitting in that repository, built by them.

Part 2: The actual gap — what was missing, in their own words

Inside that repository, there's a specific section for "Policy as Code" (the tariff-as-data idea). It had a filled-in example form (a real sample tariff) and the blank-form definition (the schema). But right there, in their own documentation, they told us — in writing — three specific things they hadn't finished deciding:

1. No way to prove who published it, or that nobody tampered with it. The form has no box for "signature." They wrote, literally: "No issuer field... do not insert an ungoverned proof field." Translation: "we know this is missing, don't just bolt something onto our form yourself."
2. No box for what currency the prices are in. They wrote: "a future schema should make currency explicit." Translation: "we haven't added this yet."
3. No agreed way to handle a correction or update to the tariff later. They wrote: "amendment convention... to be formalised." Translation: "we know we need this, we haven't written the rule yet."

That's the literal gap — not something abstract we invented, but three blank spots their own authors pointed at and said "not done yet."

We also found a fourth problem ourselves, one they hadn't written down anywhere: their form has a box for tiered pricing (cheaper for the first 100 units, pricier after) and a separate box for time-of-day surcharges (20% more expensive in the evening). But nowhere does it say: when someone's usage spans multiple tiers, is that 20% evening surcharge calculated on the cheap rate, the expensive rate, or an average? We only discovered this because we tried to actually write a program that computes a real bill from their form — and hit a fork in the road their instructions never resolved.

Part 3: What we proposed

For the three things they already knew were missing, we didn't touch their form at all — we designed a wrapper: a small labeled envelope that goes around their form, holding exactly those three missing pieces (who published it + a digital seal, what currency, and a placeholder for "this replaces an earlier version"), without erasing or editing a single box on their original form. That matters because changing someone

──────────────────────────────── can you fully read the DeG Vision Paper (pdf in the current directory and consists of 71 pages) and (Branch) ──
❯ haven't even submitted it yet — we only prepared the submission package and pushed the code publicly to GitHub. The actual government feedback channel (the form we found) hasn't been used. So right now this stands at: a real, working, independently-checkable technical result, sitting ready, waiting for you to decide whether and when to submit it.

Does this rebuild it clearly enough — or is there one specific step (the wrapper, the calculator disagreement risk, or the practice-network part) you want me to slow down on even further?
